If `track_ops.iscell_thr is None`, use binary `iscell[:,0] == 1`; otherwise use probability `iscell[:,1] > track_ops.iscell_thr`. This is important because the code’s named `dF/F0` is effectively baseline-subtracted fluorescence, not a classical ratio.\n- No trial structure, behavior variables, motion-energy extraction, or decoder code appears in `code/`. week.\n- Imaging and videography are both sampled at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition triggering the camera, so the intended reference alignment is framewise synchrony between neural and behavior streams.\n- Calcium preprocessing in the reference study:\n  - Suite2p per recording for motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction, and spike deconvolution.\n  - Cell inclusion threshold: use ROIs with Suite2p cell probability above 0.5.\n  - Downstream analyses in the paper use baseline-corrected fluorescence traces as dF/F, described as using default Suite2p parameters.\n- Behavioral preprocessing in the reference study:\n  - Motion energy is computed from consecutive video frames, as squared pixelwise differences summed over pixels, producing one scalar per frame.\n- Decoding in the reference study:\n  - Predict behavioral motion from neural population activity using ridge regression.\n  - Same-day decoding uses nested 5-fold cross-validation.\n  - Splits are consecutive 2 minute blocks.\n  - Neural and behavior traces are both slightly denoised by averaging over 10-frame bins before decoding.\n  - Performance is reported as `R^2`, with same-day decoding threshold `> 0.5`.\n- Track2p is then used to identify the subset of neurons present across all days of a mouse for longitudinal analyses.\n\n**Trial curation rules**:\n- No trial-based curation is described because the recordings are continuous spontaneous-behavior sessions rather than discrete trials.\n- Behavioral frames can be missing in some sessions; the data README says missing frames Data counts are consistent with the paper. |\n| Summary neuron counts | Code does not state paper summary statistics | Unique tracked neurons per mouse in release: `[221, 370, 685, 746, 541, 435]`, mean `499.7`, sample std `197.7` | Paper reports mean `526 ± 190` tracked neurons per mouse | Close agreement; small difference is acceptable and likely due to dataset subset / rounding in paper. |\n\nFinal understanding:\n- Source neural data are continuous 30 Hz daily recordings from barrel cortex, already matched across days within a subject.\n- The authoritative neural preprocessing target is Suite2p-style baseline-corrected fluorescence using default Suite2p parameters, because that is what the paper states for downstream analyses.\n- Behavioral motion energy is a framewise scalar aligned to imaging frames, except for missing camera frames that must be reinserted using timing gaps.\n- There is no native trial structure, so any trialization needed for the target decoder format must be an explicit downstream segmentation of continuous recordings rather than recovery of hidden experimental trials.\n\n---\n\n## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Variable Mapping\n| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n| `suite2p/plane0/F.npy`, `Fneu.npy`, `ops.npy` | `neural` | Compute Suite2p-style baseline-corrected fluorescence: `Fc = F - neucoeff * Fneu`, then `suite2p.extraction.dcnv.preprocess(...)`; average non-overlapping 10-frame bins; split continuous session into consecutive 2-minute blocks | Track2p provides loading only; paper/methods specify downstream dF/F usage | Use per-session `ops.npy` values: `neucoeff=0.7`, `baseline=maximin`, `win_baseline=60`, `sig_baseline=10`, `prctile_baseline=8`, `fs=30` |\n| Session frame index / bin centers | `input[0]` | Convert to elapsed time in seconds from session start; after 10-frame averaging, use bin-center time for each sample inside each 2-minute block | No direct reference code; required by decoder task | Time-varying single input dimension |\n| `move_deve/motion_energy_glob.npy`, `tstamps.npy`, `interframe_int.npy` | `output[0]` | Reconstruct missing frames from doubled timing gaps so behavior length matches imaging; average non-overlapping 10-frame bins; global min-max normalize valid values; discretize all valid binned samples into 5 equal-percentile bins; split into same 2-minute blocks as neural data | Paper/methods define motion energy; data README defines missing-frame handling | Single categorical output dimension with classes `Q1`-`Q5` |\n| Subject folder name (e.g. `jm031`) | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Unique subject list in sorted order; session indices point into this list | N/A | 6 subjects |\n| Constant anatomical label from paper (`barrel cortex`, layer 2/3) | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | One region label for all neurons in all sessions | Paper methods | Use one region entry for the whole dataset |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Neural trace choice**: Use paper-consistent Suite2p-style baseline-corrected fluorescence rather than raw `F`, `spks`, or the GUI `dF/F0` helper, because the paper explicitly states downstream analyses used baseline-corrected fluorescence traces with default Suite2p parameters.\n2. **Trialization strategy**: Convert each continuous session into consecutive 2-minute pseudo-trials after 10-frame averaging. This exactly matches the paper’s decoder split granularity and gives 10 trials for 20-minute sessions and 15 trials for 30-minute sessions.\n3. **Temporal binning**: Average non-overlapping 10-frame windows for neural and behavior streams before segmentation, matching the paper’s decoder denoising (`10 consecutive timestamps`). This yields `333.33 ms` bins.\n4. **Master clock**: Use imaging frames as the reference timeline because `ops.npy` gives authoritative `fs=30` and sessions have fixed imaging lengths. Reinsert missing behavior frames from timing gaps so both modalities remain frame-aligned before binning.\n5. **Behavior output representation**: Because the target decoder requires categorical outputs, convert the denoised motion-energy trace into 5 global equal-percentile classes. Use global min-max normalization only to satisfy the task wording; percentile thresholds are based on the normalized values, which preserves ordering.\n6. **Input representation**: Encode elapsed session time as one continuous, time-varying input in seconds. Each pseudo-trial keeps its absolute position within the original recording, so the decoder receives the requested “time elapsed from the beginning of the experiment.”\n7. **Session inclusion**: Keep all 41 sessions. No session fails the core reference criteria, and all sessions yield at least 10 pseudo-trials after processing.\n8. **Neuron inclusion**: Keep all neurons present in the released matched Suite2p arrays for each subject. These already represent the Track2p all-days matched population analyzed in the paper.\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [ ] Neural preprocessing spot-check: for a chosen session/neuron/window, compare converted neural values against a direct manual computation from raw `F`, `Fneu`, and `ops` using `np.allclose()`.\n- [ ] Behavior reconstruction spot-check: for a mismatch session, reconstruct the full-length motion trace directly from raw `motion_energy_glob`, `tstamps`, and `interframe_int` and verify selected samples in the converted output with `np.allclose()`.\n- [ ] Binning and segmentation check: manually average one raw 10-frame block and verify it matches the first converted time bin for neural, input time, and output class assignment.\n- [ ] Dataset statistics check: verify subject count, session count, per-subject neuron counts, and mean tracked neurons per mouse are consistent with paper-reported scale.\n\n---\n\n## Step 6: Script Development\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\nImplementation notes:\n- `convert_data.py` created.\n- Current implementation:\n  - discovers sessions from `data/`\n  - reconstructs missing behavior frames from timing gaps\n  - computes Suite2p-style baseline-corrected fluorescence using saved `ops.npy` parameters\n  - averages neural, behavior, and time streams in non-overlapping 10-frame bins\n  - segments each session into consecutive 2-minute pseudo-trials\n  - normalizes motion energy globally and discretizes it into 5 equal-percentile bins\n  - writes the target pickle format and optional processing plots\n- Smoke test passed on `--sample --show-processing`:\n  - 2 sessions processed successfully\n  - 25 pseudo-trials created\n  - 2 processing plots written\n  - mean processing time was `2.78 s / session`\n\nCode inefficiencies identified:\n- Full dataset not yet benchmarked, but the main cost is Suite2p-style fluorescence preprocessing.\n\nCode speedups added:\n- Session-wise processing only; 'jm031/'):\n- For each subject there is a folder corresponding to the subject id\n- For cross-referencing with the paper the subjects are named in alphabeticaly increasing order (e. g. jm031 - mouse A, jm032 - mouse B ... jm046 - mouse F).\n\nsession sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a'):\n- Each subject folder contains a number of session folders, each corresponding to one recording day.\n- The name of the folder corresponds to the the recording date in the YYYY-MM-DD format (the '_a' in the end of the folder name can be ignored)\n- All recordings are done daily, starting between postnatal days P7 and P9 depending on the mouse (for more information see Fig5 B in the paper)\n\nsuite2p sub-sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p'):\n- Contains the neural data for the successfully tracked neurons for the session day in Suite2p format\n- This neural data is obtained by running Track2p using the 'save the outputs in suite2p format'\n- For more information on the Suite2p format see: https://suite2p.readthedocs.io/en/latest/outputs.html\n- For more information on the Suite2p format output of Track2p see: https://track2p.github.io/outputs_suite2p.html \n- Briefly the data only includes traces for the cells present across all days, meaning that the activity matrix (for example F.npy or spks.npy, are of shape n_neurons x n_tstamps) will have the same number of rows (neurons) each day and the rows will be matched (e. g. first row of matrix 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy' corresponds to the same neuron as the first row of the matrix on the next recording day 'jm031/2023-10-19_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy')\n- This data can also be visualised using the Suite2p GUI, for example by opening two recordings of the same mouse in two windows - in that case (thanks to the reindexing) the color code will be the same across the two days for the same neuron.\n\nmove_deve sub-sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/move_deve'):\n- Contains the processed behavioural data (motion energy extracted from videography of spontaneous behaviour 'motion_energy_glob.npy')\n- In some recordings there might be some missing frames from the camera (in case that length of 'motion_energy_glob.npy' doesn't match the number of 2-photon imaging frames)\n- The indices of missing frames can be obtained by looking at 'tstamps.npy' or 'interframe_int.npy' and treated as missing values for motion energy or they can be interpolated over\n\n\n## Other data availability:\n\n- Due to space constraints we could not provide the whole raw data\n- Upon reasonable request we can provide:\n    - Raw 2-photon imaging data (.tiff files)\n    - Preprocessed Suite2p data for all detected cells on each individual days (not only the ones tracked across all days)\n    - Raw videography data of spontaneous behaviour (.avi files)\n\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

conda environment with python 3.9:\\n\\n```\\nconda create --name track2p python=3.9\\nconda activate track2p\\n```\\n\\nThen simply install the track2p package using pip:\\n\\n```\\npip install track2p\\n```\\n\\nThats it, track2p should be succesfully set up :)\\nYou can simply run it by:\\n\\n```\\npython -m track2p\\n```\\n\\nThis opens a GUI allowing the user to launch the algorithm and visualise the results interactively.\\n\\n(For instructions on running track2p without the GUI see the 'Run via script' under the 'Usage' section)\\n\\nNote: For common installation issues see ['Installation > Common issues'](https://track2p.github.io/install_common_issues.html) in documentation.\\n\\n## Reinstall\\n\\nTo reinstall, open anaconda and remove the environment with:\\n\\n```\\nconda env remove -n track2p\\n```\\n\\nThen follow the 'Installing via pip' instructions above :)\\n\\n\\n# Usage\\n\\n## Run track2p through the GUI\\n\\nAfter activating the GUI through `python -m track2p` the user should navigate to the 'Run' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Run track2p algorithm' from the dropdown menu.\\n\\nThis will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the paths to suite2p datasets and to set the algorithm parameters. After configuring these settings, the user can click 'Run' to run the track2p algorithm, and the progress will be displayed in the terminal.\\n\\nOnce the algorithm finishes a subsequent pop-up window will prompt the user to decide whether they wish to visualize the results within the interface.\\n\\nFor more details on how to run the algorithm through the GUI see [run track2p](https://track2p.github.io/run_track2p_gui.html) and for more description of parameters see documentation [parameters](https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters.html#parameters).\\n\\n## Run track2p via script\\n\\nTo run via script you can use the `run_track2p.py` script in the root of this repo as a template. It is exactly the same as running thrugh the gui, only that the paths and the parameters are defined within the script (for more on parameters etc. see documentation). When running make sure you are running it within the track2p environment, for example:\\n\\n```\\nconda activate track2p\\npython -m run_track2p\\n```\\n\\n\\n## Visualising track2p outputs within the GUI\\n\\nAfter activating the GUI through python -m track2p the user can import the results of any previous analysis by clicking on 'File' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Load processed data' from the dropdown menu. This will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the path to the track2p folder (containing the results of the algorithm) and the plane they want to open. \\n\\nOnce completed, the interface showcases multiple visualizations:\\n\\n![ex_all_vizualizations.png](docs/media/plots/ex_all_vizualizations.png)\\n\\nIn this example we are using the track2p GUI to visualise the outputs for an experiment containing 7 consecutive daily recordings in mouse barrel cortex (between P8 and P14).\\n\\nIn the upper left, the GUI visualises the mean image of the motion-corrected functional channel (usually green / GCaMP). The image is overlayed with ROIs of the cells detected by track2p across all days, with the color of a particular cell matching across days. These images are interactive, allowing the user to click on a cell, which displays the fluorescence traces on each day at the bottom of the window (sorted from the first day to the last). \\n\\nIn addition, a zoomed-in image of the cell for each day is shown in the top right. Underneath each zoomed-in image the GUI displays this cell's index in the corresponding 'suite2p’ dataset and the 'iscell' probability suite2p has assigned to it on that day.\\n\\nFinally, the user can browse all the putative matches detected by the algorithm using the bar at the bottom to toggle through matches, or alternatively they can enter the index of a specific number to display it within the GUI. This bar is also used for manual curation, where we allow the user to evaluate the quality of the tracking for each individual match.\\n\\nFor more details on how to use the GUI see [GUI usage](https://track2p.github.io/gui_overview.html).\\n\\n\\n# Outputs\\n\\nAll the outputs of the script will be saved in a `track2p` folder created within the `track_ops.save_path` directory specified by the user when running the algorithm. For an introduction on how to use the outputs for further downstream analysis we provide a useful demo notebook `demo_t2p_output.ipynb` in the root of this repository. -lc \\\"sed -n '1,220p' data/README.md\\\"\",\"aggregated_output\":\"# Data for Majnik et al. 2025:\\n\\nMajnik Jure, Mantez Manon, Zangila Sofia, Bugeon Stéphane, Guignard Leo, Platel Jean-Claude, Cossart Rosa (2025)\\nLongitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from the same cells in the developing brain using Track2p eLife 14:RP107540\\nhttps://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.1\\n\\n\\n## Programatically loading data using 'load_data.ipynb':\\n\\n- Make sure to unzip the file first, this should result in 6 folders, one for each subjects\\n- We provide a simple jupyter notebook for loading the data\\n- To load data smoothly make sure that the notebook is in the same directory as the 6 subject folders\\n- When using the dataset we suggest the users read the notes below and try in parallel to load the data using the notebook\\n\\n\\n## Notes on data organisation:\\n\\nsubject folder (e. g. 'jm031/'):\\n- For each subject there is a folder corresponding to the subject id\\n- For cross-referencing with the paper the subjects are named in alphabeticaly increasing order (e. g. jm031 - mouse A, jm032 - mouse B ... jm046 - mouse F).\\n\\nsession sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a'):\\n- Each subject folder contains a number of session folders, each corresponding to one recording day.\\n- The name of the folder corresponds to the the recording date in the YYYY-MM-DD format (the '_a' in the end of the folder name can be ignored)\\n- All recordings are done daily, starting between postnatal days P7 and P9 depending on the mouse (for more information see Fig5 B in the paper)\\n\\nsuite2p sub-sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p'):\\n- Contains the neural data for the successfully tracked neurons for the session day in Suite2p format\\n- This neural data is obtained by running Track2p using the 'save the outputs in suite2p format'\\n- For more information on the Suite2p format see: https://suite2p.readthedocs.io/en/latest/outputs.html\\n- For more information on the Suite2p format output of Track2p see: https://track2p.github.io/outputs_suite2p.html \\n- Briefly the data only includes traces for the cells present across all days, meaning that the activity matrix (for example F.npy or spks.npy, are of shape n_neurons x n_tstamps) will have the same number of rows (neurons) each day and the rows will be matched (e. g. first row of matrix 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy' corresponds to the same neuron as the first row of the matrix on the next recording day 'jm031/2023-10-19_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy')\\n- This data can also be visualised using the Suite2p GUI, for example by opening two recordings of the same mouse in two windows - in that case (thanks to the reindexing) the color code will be the same across the two days for the same neuron.\\n\\nmove_deve sub-sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/move_deve'):\\n- Contains the processed behavioural data (motion energy extracted from videography of spontaneous behaviour 'motion_energy_glob.npy')\\n- In some recordings there might be some missing frames from the camera (in case that length of 'motion_energy_glob.npy' doesn't match the number of 2-photon imaging frames)\\n- The indices of missing frames can be obtained by looking at 'tstamps.npy' or 'interframe_int.npy' and treated as missing values for motion energy or they can be interpolated over\\n\\n\\n## Other data availability:\\n\\n- Due to space constraints we could not provide the whole raw data\\n- Upon reasonable request we can provide:\\n    - In addition to the calcium indicator (GCaMP8m), we virally expressed a sparse anatomical marker (tdTomato) targeting GABAergic neurons using conditional expression in GAD67-Cre mice. This dual-labelling strategy facilitated reliable FOV identification across sessions and provided an anatomical reference for Track2p’s initial cross-session registration and tracking (Fig. 3, for comparison with tracking based on GCaMP8m registration see the ‘Benchmarking on manually tracked cells’ section). Before running Track2p, each recording was preprocessed using Suite2p for active cell detection (segmentation) and calcium fluorescence trace extraction (see Methods). The outputs of Suite2p (ROIs, traces and FOV images) were then used as inputs to the Track2p algorithm.\\n\\nWe next analyzed the development of functional properties of the tracked population of neurons across the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a minimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B for summary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, corresponding to 33 % (± 11 % std) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse. \\n\\nWe assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse movement and quantifying them using a ‘motion energy’ metric.\\n\\nChronic 2-photon calcium imaging\\n\\nLongitudinal two-photon calcium imaging was performed for each mouse for at least 6 consecutive days (see Fig. 5 for details). Imaging was performed using a Bruker (Ultima 2P) microscope with a Coherent Mai-Tai excitation laser (950 nm excitation light) and a 16x Nikon objective (NA 0.8). Before detection, emitted light was split into two optical paths each associated with a filter (red and green, 580-620 and 500-550 nm respectively), allowing us to simultaneously record the GCaMP8m and the tdTomato signal. The acquisition was performed using the Prairie view software. All recordings were performed in layer 2/3 (depth between 100-200 µm from the pial surface) with a 720 × 720 µm field of view and 512 × 512 pixel resolution. Imaging rate was 30 Hz (resonant scanner) and each session lasted 20 minutes. All experiments were performed in the dark, under sensory-minimised conditions, with mice being free to spontaneously run on a non-motorised treadmill (Luigs and Neumann). To facilitate alignment and cell tracking, we kept the alignment of the head mount with respect to the microscope fixed across all sessions for the same mouse. To record from the same region across days we manually aligned the imaging plane in x, y and z to best match the reference images of the red channel (tdTomato) from all previous recording days. During the course of each recording, pups were kept warm by a heating element mounted to the imaging setup. After each imaging session, the pups were returned to their home cage with the dam and their littermates.\\n\\nVideography\\n\\nAll videography was performed using a Basler camera (Basler ACE2 1920), with an infrared LED light source (ThorLabs 850 nm) pointed at the mouse. Videos were recorded at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple synchronisation across the two modalities.\\n\\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\\n\\nCalcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 23 for each recording separately. Suite2p additionally provides a cell classification feature, providing a probability of a classifier categorising an ROI as being a true cell. We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells. We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters) for all subsequent analyses. To facilitate the use of Track2p with other preprocessing pipelines, we implemented a data loader that takes as input three simple NumPy arrays corresponding to ROIs, field of view and traces (for more information see: https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters).\\nPreprocessing videography\\n\\nWe used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Similarly as in 62 we quantified these by looking at the pixel-wise difference of consecutive frames in the videography recording. Namely, we first took each two consecutive frames, computed their pixelwise difference. We then squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels. This yielded a scalar value quantifying the motion of the mouse at each time point, which was used for all subsequent analyses.\\n\\nFunctional properties of tracked neurons\\nCalcium event rates\\n\\nTo quantify the calcium event rate statistics we used SciPy’s peak detection algorithm (Python). For this we first denoised the traces slightly by averaging using a bin size of 10 frames and then proceeded to detect any peaks with a height and prominence of at least one standard deviation. We then calculated the rate as the number of peaks per minute within the recording. When quantifying the stability of the rates across days we computed the Pearson correlation coefficient across all neurons for each possible combination of sessions recorded from the same mouse.\\n\\nDecoding\\n\\nAll decoding was done using linear regression with ridge regularisation (ridge regression) to avoid overfitting given the large number of neurons. Ridge regression optimises the weights (β) that minimise the following loss function:\\n\\nL(\\\\beta) = \\\\|y - X\\\\beta\\\\|_2^2 + \\\\lambda\\\\|\\\\beta\\\\|_2^2\\n\\nWhere in our case y is behavioural data, X is neural data and λ is a regularisation parameter. To choose the optimal λ and to reliably estimate the model performance on same-day decoding we used nested cross-validation (see for example 65,66), ensuring data efficiency and a splitting between training, validation and test data. Briefly, a grid search for the optimal λ is performed by using the train set to fit models with different λ values and choosing the value corresponding to the model with the best performance on the held out validation set (model selection). This model is then evaluated on the test data that was used neither in model fitting nor in the hyperparameter optimization (model evaluation). To ensure data efficiency, nested cross-validation repeats this procedure for all possible splits of the data, with an outer cross-validation loop used for model evaluation and an inner loop for model selection. We used 5 fold splits for both the inner and outer loops, splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording. For cross-day decoding we fit a new model with the optimal lambda for that given day and evaluated it on all other days. For all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps. All models were implemented and fitted using PyTorch (Python).\\n\",\"exit_code\":0,\"status\":\"completed\"}}\n{\"type\":\"item.started\",\"item\":{\"id\":\"item_38\",\"type\":\"command_execution\",\"command\":\"/bin/bash -lc 'pdftotext paper.pdf - Since we tracked the same neurons across days, we could also probe the stability of this representation across days, by fitting a model on one day and testing it on all other days (cross-day decoding, Fig. 7E-H     , Fig. S7B      and C     15     ,35     ). The amount of spatial overlap after registration (yellow in Fig. 2C     , bottom), can indicate the accuracy of the estimated transformation between the two days. Assuming that the transformation is accurately estimated, ROIs corresponding to the same cells display substantial spatial overlap, with some ROIs from one recording also potentially overlapping poorly, if not at all, with any ROI from another. Thirdly, once the ROIs are aligned, the algorithm proceeds with the matching (Fig. 2D     ). This is done by computing a spatial similarity metric (intersection over union - IoU) between each ROI from session s0 and each transformed ROI from session s1. Functional properties of tracked neurons Calcium event rates To quantify the calcium event rate statistics we used SciPy’s peak detection algorithm (Python). For this we first denoised the traces slightly by averaging using a bin size of 10 frames and then proceeded to detect any peaks with a height and prominence of at least one standard deviation. We then calculated the rate as the number of peaks per minute within the recording. When quantifying the stability of the rates across days we computed the Pearson correlation coefficient across all neurons for each possible combination of sessions recorded from the same mouse. Pairwise correlations and PCA We quantified pairwise correlations by computing the Pearson correlation between the full traces of all pairs of simultaneously recorded neurons within each session. To quantify the spatial properties of pairwise correlations, we additionally calculated the euclidean distance between ROI Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 22 of 46 centroids for each corresponding pair of neurons. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a minimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B      for summary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, corresponding to 33 % (± 11 % std) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse. Based on the high tracking performance on ground truth (see Fig 4     ) we conclude that this drop is due to a failure in detection rather than tracking. During the course of the experiment, the weight of a mouse increased on average by 55% (± 18% std), with the pairwise distance between neurons increasing on average by 15% (± 7% std) (Fig. 5C      and Fig. S5F     ). Weight was found to vary significantly across mice, as early as the first experimental day, and to correlate with brain growth (r=0.92, Fig. S5E     ). To facilitate the use of Track2p with other preprocessing pipelines, we implemented a data loader that takes as input three simple NumPy arrays corresponding to ROIs, field of view and traces (for more information see: https:// track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters     ). Preprocessing videography We used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Similarly as in 62      we quantified these by looking at the pixel-wise difference of consecutive frames in the videography recording. Namely, we first took each two consecutive frames, computed their pixelwise difference. We then squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels. This yielded a scalar value quantifying the motion of the mouse at each tim\\n\\nTERM: barrel cortex -> 1480\\nlongitudinal studies, automated tools for reliably tracking large populations of neurons across sessions remain limited. Here, we present a novel cell-tracking method based on sequential image registration, validated on calcium imaging data from the barrel cortex of mouse pups over one postnatal week. Our approach enables robust long-term analysis of several hundreds of individual neurons, allowing quantification of neuronal dynamics and representational stability over time. Using this method, we identified a key developmental transition in neuronal activity statistics, marking the emergence of arousal state modulation. Beyond this key finding, our method provides an essential tool for tracking developmental trajectories of individual neurons, which could help identify potential deviations associated with neurodevelopmental disorders. Introduction Early postnatal development in rodents is a period of intense circuit wiring and remodelling at several scales through various major processes that include neuronal growth, synaptogenesis, apoptosis, migration, rise of intracortical connectivity, functional maturation of inhibitory Reviewe\\n\\nTERM: 20 minutes -> 58163\\nhe Prairie view software. All recordings were performed in layer 2/3 (depth between 100-200 µm from the pial surface) with a 720 × 720 µm field of view and 512 × 512 pixel resolution. Imaging rate was 30 Hz (resonant scanner) and each session lasted 20 minutes. All experiments were performed in the dark, under sensory-minimised conditions, with mice being free to spontaneously run on a non- motorised treadmill (Luigs and Neumann). To facilitate alignment and cell tracking, we kept the alignment of the head mount with respect to the microscope fixed across all sessions for the same mouse. To record from the same region across days we manually aligned the imaging plane in x, y and z to best match the reference images of the red channel (tdTomato) from all previous Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 19 of 46 recording days. During the course of each recording, pups were kept warm by a heating element mounted to the imaging setup. After each imaging session, the pups were returned to their home cage with the dam and their littermates. Videography All videography was performed using a Basler camera (Ba\\n\\nTERM: 30 Hz -> 59178\\nere returned to their home cage with the dam and their littermates. Videography All videography was performed using a Basler camera (Basler ACE2 1920), with an infrared LED light source (ThorLabs 850 nm) pointed at the mouse. Videos were recorded at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple synchronisation across the two modalities. All acquisition was done using custom python scripts using the PyPylon wrapper for the Basler camera software (Pylon Camera Software Suite, https:// github.com/basler/pypylon     ). Processing of calcium imaging data Calcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 23      for each recording separately. Suite2p additionally provides a cell classification feature, providing a probability of a classifier categorising an ROI as being a true cell. We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells. We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters) for all\\n\\nTERM: nested cross-validation -> 69880\\nweights (β) that minimise the following loss function: Where in our case y is behavioural data, X is neural data and λ is a regularisation parameter. To choose the optimal λ and to reliably estimate the model performance on same-day decoding we used nested cross-validation (see for example 65     ,66     ), ensuring data efficiency and a splitting between training, validation and test data. Briefly, a grid search for the optimal λ is performed by using the train set to fit models with different λ values and choosing the value corresponding to the model with the best performance on the held out validation set (model selection). This model is then evaluated on the test data that was used neither in model fitting nor in the hyperparameter optimization (model evaluation). To ensure data efficiency, nested cross-validation repeats this procedure for all possible splits of the data, with an outer cross-validation loop used for model evaluation and an inner loop for model selection. We used 5 fold splits for both the inner and outer loops, splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording. E: Box plots comparing within day (left) or across-day (right) FC similarity for early (≤ P11) and late (> P11) developmental epochs pooled across all mice. *: statistically significant; ns: not statistically significant (Kruskall-Wallis test: p = 3.6 × 10−22; post-hoc Mann–Whitney U tests with Bonferroni correction, early within - late within: p = 3.3 × 10−3, early to early - late to late: p = n. s., early to early - early to late: p = 1.7 × 10−12, late to late - early to late: p = 4.2 × 10−5, for all possible comparisons see Fig. S6K     ). Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 14 of 46 Altogether, our analyses indicate that the second postnatal week marks a transition between two stable functional network structures in the barrel cortex of developing mice. Additionally, this shows how tracking cells during development opens new analysis possibilities using self- referencing between neurons, provi\\n\\nTERM: same-day -> 42069\\n are more strongly modulated by active movement at later than earlier developmental stages. Finally, we asked whether behavioral state could be decoded from population activity dynamics using regression analysis. Despite substantial variability, the same-day decoding performance increased with development (Fig. 7A-C     ). Since we tracked the same neurons across days, we could also probe the stability of this representation across days, by fitting a model on one day and testing it on all other days (cross-day decoding, Fig. 7E-H     , Fig. S7B      and C     15     ,35     ). Interestingly, this analysis showed that, once developed, this representation was indeed stable, allowing for accurate cross-day decoding, with the same neurons showing either consistently positive or negative modulation across days (see Fig. S7D      for the activity traces of an example cell with a high weight for decoding). Hence, by combining Track2p longitudinal cell tracking with decoding approaches, one can systematically map the emergence and stability of cortical representations in the developing brain. Here, we present a novel cell-tracking method based on sequential image registration, validated on calcium imaging data from the barrel cortex of mouse pups over one postnatal week. Our approach enables robust long-term analysis of several hundreds of individual neurons, allowing quantification of neuronal dynamics and representational stability over time. Using this method, we identified a key developmental transition in neuronal activity statistics, marking the emergence of arousal state modulation. Beyond this key finding, our method provides an essential tool for tracking developmental trajectories of individual neurons, which could help identify potential deviations associated with neurodevelopmental disorders. Interestingly, this sorting revealed a subpopulation of neurons that were highly correlated with the animal’s motion at later, but not earlier developmental stages (Fig. 5A     , see Fig. S4A      for all days). We first quantified this relationship by computing the temporal correlation between the first principal component of neuronal activity with the animal’s motion. Interestingly, this correlation showed a steep increase after P11 for most mice (Fig. 7D     ). This suggested that global fluctuations in neural activity are more strongly modulated by active movement at later than earlier developmental stages. Finally, we asked whether behavioral state could be decoded from population activity dynamics using regression analysis. Despite substantial variability, the same-day decoding performance increased with development (Fig. 7A-C     ). Hence, by combining Track2p longitudinal cell tracking with decoding approaches, one can systematically map the emergence and stability of cortical representations in the developing brain. Discussion Here we described Track2P, a cell tracking algorithm that can be used to follow the changing dynamics of hundreds of matched neurons from daily calcium imaging recordings in the growing brain of living mouse pups. At the core of the method lies sequential affine registration followed by cell matching for each sub\\n\\nTERM Figure 7 43983\\ned activity to multidimensional, behavior-state dependent neural dynamics. Beyond this key finding, our method opens a much needed tool for investigating the developmental functional trajectories of individual neurons during early postnatal brain development, and their deviations caused by genetic mutations or environmental perturbations. Since we tracked the same neurons across days, we could also probe the stability of this representation across days, by fitting a model on one day and testing it on all other days (cross-day decoding, Fig. 7E-H     , Fig. S7B      and C     15     ,35     ). Interestingly, this analysis showed that, once developed, this representation was indeed stable, allowing for accurate cross-day decoding, with the same neurons showing either consistently positive or negative modulation across days (see Fig. S7D      for the activity traces of an example cell with a high weight for decoding). Hence, by combining Track2p longitudinal cell tracking with decoding approaches, one can systematically map the emergence and stability of cortical representations in the developing brain. Discussion Here we described Track2P, a cell tracking algorithm that can be used to follow the changing dynamics of hundreds of matched neurons from daily calcium imaging recordings in the growing brain of living mouse pups. At the core of the method lies sequential affine registration followed by cell matching for each subsequent pair of recordings, which lead to a better tracking performance during development when using Track2p compared to other methods. Using this benchmarked approach, we observed a sharp developmental transition from highly synchronized activity to multidimensional, behavior-state dependent neural dynamics. _train_decoder_pca_logistic(neural: list, input: list, output: list, metadata: dict = {}, **kwargs):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Trains a common decoder for all sessions to predict output from neural and input data.\\n    Concatenates data across trials for each session, then projects the neural data for each session onto its first npcs principal components.\\n    Then, concatenates data across all sessions and trains a common decoder across all sessions using logistic regression to predict output from the\\n    projected neural data and input data.\\n\\n    Inputs:\\n        neural: neural activity. neural is a list of length nsessions, neural[session] is a list\\n                of length ntrials[session], and neural[session][trial] is a numpy array of shape (nneurons[session], T[session][trial]),\\n                dtype = float32, giving the neural activity for that trial\\n        input: variables beyond neural activity the decoder should input, e.g. the stimulis, condition. input is a list of\\n                length nsessions, input[session] is a list of length ntrials[session], and input[session][trial] is a numpy array of\\n                shape (dinput, T[session][trial]), dtype = float32. with the input variable(s) for each timepoint.\\n                dinput is the number of inputs. For discrete inputs, use a one-hot encoding.\\n        output: variables we want to decode. output is a list of length nsessions, output[session] is a list of length ntrials[session],\\n                and output[session][trial] is a numpy array of shape (doutput, T[session][trial]) of dtype = bool with the\\n                binary output variable(s) for each timepoint. Use a one-hot encoding for multi-class outputs.\\n        metadata: optional dict with additional information about the experiment.\\n\\n        Algorithm hyperparameters can be passed as keyword arguments:\\n        npcs: number of principal components to use (default: 10)\\n\\n    Returns:\\n        model: dict with trained decoder parameters. dict with the keys:\\n            'pca': list of length nsessions, pca[session] is a PCA object fitted to the neural data of that session\\n            'logisticregression': list of length doutput, logisticregression[out_dim] is a sklearn LogisticRegression model\\n\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\n    model = {\\n        'pca': [],\\n        'logisticregression': []\\n    }\\n\\n    npcs = kwargs.get('npcs', 10)\\n    nsessions = len(neural)\\n\\n    # Replicate 1D inputs/outputs along time axis to match neural data (make copies to avoid modifying original)\\n    input_processed = []\\n    output_processed = []\\n    for session in range(nsessions):\\n        input_session = []\\n        output_session = []\\n        for trial in range(len(neural[session])):\\n            T = neural[session][trial].shape[1]  # time dimension\\n\\n            # Handle 1D input - replicate along time\\n            if input[session][trial].ndim == 1:\\n                input_trial = np.tile(input[session][trial][:, np.newaxis], (1, T))\\n            else:\\n                input_trial = input[session][trial]\\n            input_session.append(input_trial)\\n\\n            # Handle 1D output - replicate along time\\n            if output[session][trial].ndim == 1:\\n                output_trial = np.tile(output[session][trial][:, np.newaxis], (1, T))\\n            else:\\n                output_trial = output[session][trial]\\n            output_session.append(output_trial)\\n\\n        input_processed.append(input_session)\\n        output_processed.append(output_session)\\n\\n    # Use processed data instead of original\\n    input = input_processed\\n    output = output_processed\\n\\n    # Store projected neural data and inputs/outputs for all sessions\\n    all_projected_neural = []\\n    all_input = []\\n    all_output = []\\n\\n    # Process each session separately\\n    for session in range(nsessions):\\n        # Concatenate across trials for this session\\n        # neural[session][trial] is (nneurons, T), we want (T_total, nneurons)\\n        neural_concat = np.concatenate([neural[session][trial].T for trial in range(len(neural[session]))], axis=0).astype(np.float32)\\n        input_concat = np.concatenate([input[session][trial].T for trial in range(len(input[session]))], axis=0).astype(np.float32)\\n        output_concat = np.concatenate([output[session][trial].T for trial in range(len(output[session]))], axis=0).astype(bool)\\n\\n        # {"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_26","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,220p' /app/cache/paper_text.txt\"","aggregated_output":"Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 1 of 46\nNeuroscience\nLongitudinal tracking of neuronal\nactivity from the same cells in the\ndeveloping brain using Track2p\nJure Majnik , Manon Mantez, Sofia Zangila, Stéphane Bugeon, Leo Guignard, Jean-Claude Platel,\nRosa Cossart\nAix Marseille Univ, Inserm, INMED, Turing Center for Living Systems, Marseille, France • Aix-Marseille Univ, CNRS,\nIBDM, UMR7288, Turing Centre for Living Systems, Marseille, France\nhttps://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_access\nCopyright information\neLife Assessment\nThis fundamental study presents a new method for longitudinally tracking cells in\ntwo-photon imaging data that addresses the specific challenges of imaging neurons\nin the developing cortex. It provides compelling evidence demonstrating reliable\nlongitudinal identification of neurons across the second postnatal week in mice. The\nstudy should be of interest to development neuroscientists engaged in population-\nlevel recordings using two-photon imaging.\nhttps://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2.sa4\nAbstract\nUnderstanding cortical circuit development requires tracking neuronal activity across days in\nthe growing brain. While in vivo calcium imaging now enables such longitudinal studies,\nautomated tools for reliably tracking large populations of neurons across sessions remain\nlimited. Here, we present a novel cell-tracking method based on sequential image\nregistration, validated on calcium imaging data from the barrel cortex of mouse pups over\none postnatal week. Our approach enables robust long-term analysis of several hundreds of\nindividual neurons, allowing quantification of neuronal dynamics and representational\nstability over time. Using this method, we identified a key developmental transition in\nneuronal activity statistics, marking the emergence of arousal state modulation. Beyond this\nkey finding, our method provides an essential tool for tracking developmental trajectories of\nindividual neurons, which could help identify potential deviations associated with\nneurodevelopmental disorders.\nIntroduction\nEarly postnatal development in rodents is a period of intense circuit wiring and remodelling at\nseveral scales through various major processes that include neuronal growth, synaptogenesis,\napoptosis, migration, rise of intracortical connectivity, functional maturation of inhibitory\nReviewed Preprint\nv2 • October 27, 2025\nRevised by authors\nReviewed Preprint\nv1 • July 9, 2025\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 2 of 46\nsynapses and the disappearance of transient connectivity schemes 1     –4     . Critical periods for\nvarious sensory systems open and close during this time, further highlighting the profound\nreshaping of cortical networks 4     . This developmental period of remodelling is crucial for\nestablishing the functional architecture of the mature cortex. Most importantly, all of these\ndevelopmental processes are activity-dependent, with collective dynamics playing a critical role in\nthe proper integration of neurons into functional networks 5     . These network dynamics\nsequentially unfold while relying on different mechanisms and circuits for their generation\n1     ,5     . The emergence of functional brain circuits during development is therefore a precisely\ntimed choreography, the timing of which is inherently tied to the age of the organism under study.\nHowever, developmental age lacks precision due to significant variations in physical\ncharacteristics and growth patterns, even among offspring from the same genetic lineage (Fig.\n1     ). Hence, longitudinal imaging from the same animal across days is the optimal solution to\nbetter capture the evolution of circuit dynamics during development. Additionally, developmental\nvariability extends beyond the organism level, becoming even more pronounced at the single\nneuron level (Fig. 1     ). While population-level descriptions of cortical circuit development are\ncrucial, individual neurons exhibit unique developmental trajectories rooted in their specific\norigin, birth timing and cellular identity 1     . In addition, the singular dynamics of sparse\nindividual neurons can matter, as demonstrated for the rare hub cells 6     ,7     . Thus, in order to\nfully understand the circuit basis of cortical development in health and disease, it is crucial to\ntrack neuronal activity at both population and individual cell levels in the growing brain of the\nsame animal. This hurdle spans both experimental methodology and data analysis.\nSignificant progress has been made over the recent years to develop innovative solutions to meet\nthis challenge on the experimental side, using two-photon calcium imaging. Technical\nimprovements included modified head plates and specialized surgical and care protocols 6     ,8     –\n12     . Still, in many cases, different neurons from the same mouse were recorded at different ages,\nor the tracking of individual cells could only be achieved through visual inspection and manual\nannotation of relatively sparse, often genetically-subscribed neuronal populations expressing a\ncalcium fluorescent reporter. The gold standard for developmental circuit analysis would be\nautomated tracking of densely labeled neuronal populations, enabling efficient longitudinal\nmonitoring while eliminating the burden of manual cell tracking. An algorithm capable of\naccurate automatic tracking of the same neurons across multiple days of brain development is\nthus essential.\nMethods for tracking the activity of large populations of cells across days have been successfully\ndeveloped and deployed in the adult brain 13     –16     . In adult circuits, cell tracking is indeed\nrelatively straightforward due to their structural stability, with minimal tissue growth, negligible\nmorphological changes, and constant numbers of neurons. In contrast, the first weeks of mouse\nneocortical development are characterized by rapid and critical developmental changes spanning\nseveral scales from synapses, to single neurons and networks. These include extensive brain\ngrowth 17     ,18     , substantial morphological changes 19      as well as changes to cell numbers due\nto programmed cell death 10     . This makes tracking the same cells across sessions substantially\nmore difficult during development compared to the adult brain.\nTo overcome the technical limitations detailed above, we developed an experimental protocol\nusing chronic calcium imaging in mice and a novel cell tracking algorithm (Track2p), specifically\ntailored to development. This allowed us to track the activity of large, densely labelled,\npopulations of neurons during early postnatal development, which has not been possible before.\nThe algorithm overcomes the challenges of cell tracking during brain growth by applying\nsequential registration and cell matching steps, using the spatial overlap of cells on adjacent\nrecordings as a matching criterion. Track2p is freely available as an open-source package with an\ninteractive GUI, enabling researchers to analyze longitudinal calcium imaging data from both\ndeveloping and mature circuits.\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 3 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 3 of 46\nFigure 1\nTracking developmental trajectories at the organismal and cellular level.\nPostnatal development in mammals is not a strictly stereotyped process (left) but rather shows variability across individual\norganisms as well as across individual cells of the same organism (right). Performing an acute experiment (dashed grey line)\nonly provides a single snapshot of the developmental trajectory of an individual organism (or cell). Alternatively, a\nlongitudinal experiment (grey arrow) allows to track the properties of the same individual (or cell) throughout development,\nwhich is especially important in the case of variability in developmental trajectories (right).\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 4 of 46\nApplying the algorithm to a dataset recorded during the second postnatal week in mouse barrel\ncortex, a critical period for the formation of topographic maps in that region, yielded hundreds of\nidentified neurons tracked across all days. Assessing the quality of the algorithm by benchmarking\nit on a newly generated ground truth dataset showed high tracking performance 20     . Leveraging\nground truth benchmarking, we demonstrate that explicitly accounting for developmental\nprocesses, such as brain growth, are critical for accurately tracking cells during postnatal\ndevelopment. Our work thus shows that chronic calcium imaging and cell tracking using Track2p\ncan be used to monitor the changing physiological properties of large populations of matched\nneurons during early cortical development. We demonstrate that the statistics of activity patterns\nin the tracked population display two periods of stability, with a critical transition point around\npostnatal day 11 (P11), marking the emergence of a stable behavioral state representation.\nResults\nCell tracking using image registration and overlap-based matching\nTracking neuronal activity across multiple days presents unique challenges due to the dynamic\nnature of brain development. We developed a novel tracking algorithm, called Track2p. As in\nother tracking algorithms for calcium imaging data 13     ,16     ,21     , the final goal of Track2p is to\nfollow individual cells across sessions allowing the user to compare their functional properties in\ndownstream analyses. To achieve this goal, the algorithm takes as input a set of preprocessed\nrecordings, each consisting of a set of regions of interest (ROIs i.e. putative neurons detected based\non activity, see Methods) and their respective calcium fluorescence traces, as well as a mean image\nof the field of view (FOV). Briefly, the algorithm aims to match ROIs in any given pair of\nconsecutive sessions based on their spatial overlap, assuming that the more the two overlap in\nanatomical space, the more likely they correspond to the same neuron. Due to developmental\nprocesses such as brain growth and other experimental factors, it is necessary to account for day-\nto-day changes that occur between the two recordings before computing spatial overlaps. This is\nachieved by performing affine image registration on the mean FOV images between consecutive\ndays.\nWe apply the registration and spatial matching iteratively, starting with the first pair of sessions\n(s0 and s1) as follows (Fig. 2A     ):\nFirstly, we estimate the spatial transformation between s0 and s1 using affine image registration\n(Ntatsis 2023) (i.e. allowing shifting, rotation, scaling and shearing, see Fig. 2B     , the\ntransformation is denoted as T). We employ affine transformation, since it can account both for\nrigid transformations (rotations and translations arising from minor mismatches in FOV\nalignment across experiments) as well as scaling and shearing (mostly due to brain growth). The\nchanges across the two consecutive recordings are approximated as the transformation\nregistering the mean FOV from session s1 (green in Fig. 2B     ) to the mean FOV of s0 serving as\nreference (red in Fig. 2B     ).\nSecondly, the computed transformation is applied to the ROIs from session s1 (green in Fig. 2C     )\nto align them to ROIs from session s0 (red in Fig. 2C     ). The amount of spatial overlap after\nregistration (yellow in Fig. 2C     , bottom), can indicate the accuracy of the estimated\ntransformation between the two days. Assuming that the transformation is accurately estimated,\nROIs corresponding to the same cells display substantial spatial overlap, with some ROIs from one\nrecording also potentially overlapping poorly, if not at all, with any ROI from another.\nThirdly, once the ROIs are aligned, the algorithm proceeds with the matching (Fig. 2D     ). This is\ndone by computing a spatial similarity metric (intersection over union - IoU) between each ROI\nfrom session s0 and each transformed ROI from session s1. Matches are then assigned in a globally\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 5 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 5 of 46\nFigure 2\nThe Track2p pipeline for tracking cells across recordings.\nA: Schematic overview of the Track2p algorithm and the GUI capabilities. Dashed squares represent optional steps in the\npipeline. B: The transformation (T) between two consecutive days is computed by registering the mean FOV images. Top: An\noverlay of the reference (red, s0) and the moving (green, s1) images before registration. Bottom: Same two images after\nregistration. Scale bar: 50 μm. C: Applying the transform to ROIs from Suite2p segmentation. The ROI color code is the same\nas in B, the intersection of the ROIs is shown in yellow. Top: Overlap before T. Bottom: Overlap after T. Note: Only a few\nexample ROIs are displayed for explanation purposes. D: Cell matching using linear sum assignment. ROIs are the same as C\nwith indexes for the two recordings added for illustrative purposes. Cells from one recording are matched to cells from\nanother maximising the sum of the intersection over union (IoU) across all matches. E: Filtering putative false and true\nmatches by thresholding the IoU distribution. Top: The distribution of IoU values for matched ROI pairs shows a bimodal\ndistribution, which is used to reject putative false positive matches. Bottom: Final result of the cell linking for a pair of\nrecordings. F: Top: In the case of tracking across more than 2 imaging sessions, the steps from B to D are repeated\nsequentially to link the cells across all days. Bottom: Example matches for five cells (rows) successfully tracked across five\nconsecutive days (columns). Note: The figures shown are for illustrative purposes only, see Fig. 3      for application to real\ndata.\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 6 of 46\noptimal way by maximising the sum of IoU values across all matches using a linear sum\nassignment algorithm (Crouse 2016).\nFinally, since two consecutive sessions contain different sets of detected cells (see * in Fig. 2D     ,\nbottom) and since ROIs can overlap even if the signal does not come from the same cells (see + in\nFig. 2D     , bottom) we perform an additional filtering step on the assigned matches. Assuming that\nthe IoU values for putative true and putative false matches come from different distributions, we\nwould expect a bimodal distribution of IoU values across all assigned matches (see histogram in\nFig. 2E     ). To reject the putative false positives, we compute a threshold based on automatic\nthresholding methods (Otsu’s method 24     ). This ensures that assigned matches with low spatial\nsimilarity are rejected (see + in Fig. 2D and E     ) while the matches with high similarity are\naccepted (see x in Fig. 2D and E     ). This yields the final matching for the first pair of consecutive\nimaging sessions (s0 and s1). In the case of more than 2 recordings, this tracking procedure is then\niteratively applied for all consecutive pairs of sessions (s0 to s1, s1 to s2 … sN-1 to sN, and so on),\nwith tracks being extended sequentially from s0 to sN based on the identified matches, and\nterminated if a match was not identified at any particular session (Fig. 2F     ).\nWe provide an open-source implementation of the algorithm, combined with a user-friendly\ngraphical user interface (GUI), allowing non-specialist users to run the algorithm and interact with\nits outputs (see Fig. 2A      top, Fig. S1      and Supp. Info.: Software). Both the algorithm and the GUI\ncome with a simple installation procedure and extensive documentation to facilitate the ease of\nuse and accessibility of Track2p.\nTracking neurons across days in the early postnatal neocortex\nIn order to validate our method on experimental data, we next applied Track2p to a longitudinal\ndataset consisting of daily recordings of the same 720×720 μm FOV throughout the second\npostnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development (postnatal days 8 (P8) to P14, n=7 imaging\nsessions from one mouse; for more details see Methods.) In addition to the calcium indicator\n(GCaMP8m), we virally expressed a sparse anatomical marker (tdTomato) targeting GABAergic\nneurons using conditional expression in GAD67-Cre mice 22     . This dual-labelling strategy\nfacilitated reliable FOV identification across sessions and provided an anatomical reference for\nTrack2p’s initial cross-session registration and tracking (Fig. 3     , for comparison with tracking\nbased on GCaMP8m registration see the ‘Benchmarking on manually tracked cells’ section). Before\nrunning Track2p, each recording was preprocessed using Suite2p 23      for active cell detection\n(segmentation) and calcium fluorescence trace extraction (see Methods). The outputs of Suite2p\n(ROIs, traces and FOV images) were then used as inputs to the Track2p algorithm.\nThe Track2p algorithm appeared to successfully register the mean FOV for each successive pair of\nimaging sessions (based on visual inspection of Track2p outputs; for the first and last pairs see Fig.\n3A and B      bottom left; for the equivalent of Fig. 3      across all days see Fig. S2A     ). Registering\nthe ROIs for each pair of consecutive recordings showed a great degree of overlap for the majority\nof ROIs (yellow area in Fig. 3A and B      bottom). We did however observe several ROIs that were\nonly present in one session from the pair, likely due to differences in cell detection and\ndevelopmental factors such as growth, silencing or cell death. When matching ROIs across sessions\nusing spatial overlap (IoU), we would expect these ROIs to show significantly lower IoU values\ncompared to the cells that were present in both sessions. Indeed, the IoU distribution of assigned\nmatches revealed a bimodal distribution for each pair of imaging sessions, allowing us to use\nclassical histogram thresholding methods 24     , clearly separating the distributions of putative true\nand false matches (Fig. 3A and B      bottom right, Fig. S2B     ). Propagating the putative matches\nyielded a total of 728 ROIs that were tracked across all days in this example mouse (out of 1988\nROIs detected in the first recording, Fig. 3E     ). We used the subset of cells successfully tracked\nacross all days for all our future analyses.\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 7 of 46Jure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2 7 of 46\nFigure 3\nTrack2p tracks hundreds of cells throughout the second postnatal week\nof development in the mouse neocortex despite substantial brain growth.\nA: Top left and right: Mean images of the ‘anatomical’ channel (tdTomato in GABAergic cells) for the first two imaging days\n(P8 and P9). Interestingly, this sorting revealed a subpopulation of neurons that were highly correlated with the animal’s motion at later, but not earlier developmental stages (Fig. 5A     , see Fig. S4A      for all days). Finally, we asked whether behavioral state could be decoded from population activity dynamics using regression analysis. Despite substantial variability, the same-day decoding performance increased with development (Fig. 7A-C     ). Since we tracked the same neurons across days, we could also probe the stability of this representation across days, by fitting a model on one day and testing it on all other days (cross-day decoding, Fig. 7E-H     , Fig. S7B      and C     15     ,35     ). Interestingly, this analysis showed that, once developed, this representation was indeed stable, allowing for accurate cross-day decoding, with the same neurons showing either consistently positive or negative modulation across days (see Fig. S7D      for the activity traces of an example cell with a high weight for decoding). Hence, by combining Track2p longitudinal cell tracking with decoding approaches, one can systematically map the emergence and stability of cortical representations in the developing brain. Videography All videography was performed using a Basler camera (Basler ACE2 1920), with an infrared LED light source (ThorLabs 850 nm) pointed at the mouse. Videos were recorded at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple synchronisation across the two modalities. Processing of calcium imaging data Calcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 23      for each recording separately. Preprocessing videography We used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Similarly as in 62      we quantified these by looking at the pixel-wise difference of consecutive frames in the videography recording. Namely, we first took each two consecutive frames, computed their pixelwise difference. We then squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels. This yielded a scalar value quantifying the motion of the mouse at each time point, which was used for all subsequent analyses. Cell tracking Image \n\nTERM fluorescence 9815\nllow individual cells across sessions allowing the user to compare their functional properties in downstream analyses. To achieve this goal, the algorithm takes as input a set of preprocessed recordings, each consisting of a set of regions of interest (ROIs i.e. putative neurons detected based on activity, see Methods) and their respective calcium fluorescence traces, as well as a mean image of the field of view (FOV). Briefly, the algorithm aims to match ROIs in any given pair of consecutive sessions based on their spatial overlap, assuming that the more the two overlap in anatomical space, the more likely they correspond to the same neuron. Due to developmental processes such as brain growth and other experimental factors, it is necessary to account for day- to-day changes that occur between the two recordings before computing spatial overlaps. This is achieved by performing affine image registration on the mean FOV images between consecutive days. We apply the registration and spatial matching iteratively, starting with the first pair of sessions (s0 and s1) as follows (Fig. 2A     ): Firstly, we estimate the spatial transformation between s0 and s1 using affine image registration (Ntatsis 2023) (i.e. allowing shifting, rotatio\n\nTERM motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 59642\nsynchronisation across the two modalities. All acquisition was done using custom python scripts using the PyPylon wrapper for the Basler camera software (Pylon Camera Software Suite, https:// github.com/basler/pypylon     ). Processing of calcium imaging data Calcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 23      for each recording separately. Suite2p additionally provides a cell classification feature, providing a probability of a classifier categorising an ROI as being a true cell. We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells. We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters) for all subsequent analyses. To facilitate the use of Track2p with other preprocessing pipelines, we implemented a data loader that takes as input three simple NumPy arrays corresponding to ROIs, field of view and traces (for more information see: https:// track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters     ). Preprocessing videography We used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Similarly as in 62      we quantified these by looking at the \n\nTERM ridge regression 69525\n inflection point (‘transition age’) and the linear portion (‘transition time’) of the sigmoid using the extrema of the first and fourth derivatives respectively. For principal component analysis (PCA) we used the scikit-learn implementation (Pedregosa 2012, Python). Decoding All decoding was done using linear regression with ridge regularisation (ridge regression) to avoid overfitting given the large number of neurons. Ridge regression optimises the weights (β) that minimise the following loss function: Where in our case y is behavioural data, X is neural data and λ is a regularisation parameter. To choose the optimal λ and to reliably estimate the model performance on same-day decoding we used nested cross-validation (see for example 65     ,66     ), ensuring data efficiency and a splitting between training, validation and test data. Briefly, a grid search for the optimal λ is performed by using the train set to fit models with different λ values and choosing the value corresponding to the model with the best performance on the held out validation set (model selection). This model is then evaluated on the test data that was used neither in model fitting nor in the hyperparameter optimization (model evaluation). To ensure data e\n\nTERM same-day decoding 42069\ne after P11 for most mice (Fig. 7D     ). This suggested that global fluctuations in neural activity are more strongly modulated by active movement at later than earlier developmental stages. Finally, we asked whether behavioral state could be decoded from population activity dynamics using regression analysis. Despite substantial variability, the same-day decoding performance increased with development (Fig. 7A-C     ). Since we tracked the same neurons across days, we could also probe the stability of this representation across days, by fitting a model on one day and testing it on all other days (cross-day decoding, Fig. 7E-H     , Fig. S7B      and C     15     ,35     ). Interestingly, this analysis showed that, once developed, this representation was indeed stable, allowing for accurate cross-day decoding, with the same neurons showing either consistently positive or negative modulation across days (see Fig. S7D      for the activity traces of an example cell with a high weight for decoding). Hence, by combining Track2p longitudinal cell tracking with decoding approaches, one can systematically map the emergence and stability of cortical representations in the developing brain. In addition to the calcium indicator (GCaMP8m), we virally expressed a sparse anatomical marker (tdTomato) targeting GABAergic neurons using conditional expression in GAD67-Cre mice. This dual-labelling strategy facilitated reliable FOV identification across sessions and provided an anatomical reference for Track2p’s initial cross-session registration and tracking (Fig. 3, for comparison with tracking based on GCaMP8m registration see the ‘Benchmarking on manually tracked cells’ section). Before running Track2p, each recording was preprocessed using Suite2p for active cell detection (segmentation) and calcium fluorescence trace extraction (see Methods). The outputs of Suite2p (ROIs, traces and FOV images) were then used as inputs to the Track2p algorithm.\n\nWe next analyzed the development of functional properties of the tracked population of neurons across the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a minimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B for summary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, corresponding to 33 % (± 11 % std) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse. \n\nWe assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse movement and quantifying them using a ‘motion energy’ metric.\n\nChronic 2-photon calcium imaging\n\nLongitudinal two-photon calcium imaging was performed for each mouse for at least 6 consecutive days (see Fig. 5 for details). Imaging was performed using a Bruker (Ultima 2P) microscope with a Coherent Mai-Tai excitation laser (950 nm excitation light) and a 16x Nikon objective (NA 0.8). Before detection, emitted light was split into two optical paths each associated with a filter (red and green, 580-620 and 500-550 nm respectively), allowing us to simultaneously record the GCaMP8m and the tdTomato signal. The acquisition was performed using the Prairie view software. All recordings were performed in layer 2/3 (depth between 100-200 µm from the pial surface) with a 720 × 720 µm field of view and 512 × 512 pixel resolution. Imaging rate was 30 Hz (resonant scanner) and each session lasted 20 minutes. All experiments were performed in the dark, under sensory-minimised conditions, with mice being free to spontaneously run on a non-motorised treadmill (Luigs and Neumann). To facilitate alignment and cell tracking, we kept the alignment of the head mount with respect to the microscope fixed across all sessions for the same mouse. To record from the same region across days we manually aligned the imaging plane in x, y and z to best match the reference images of the red channel (tdTomato) from all previous recording days. During the course of each recording, pups were kept warm by a heating element mounted to the imaging setup. After each imaging session, the pups were returned to their home cage with the dam and their littermates.\n\nVideography\n\nAll videography was performed using a Basler camera (Basler ACE2 1920), with an infrared LED light source (ThorLabs 850 nm) pointed at the mouse. Videos were recorded at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple synchronisation across the two modalities.\n\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\n\nCalcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 23 for each recording separately. Suite2p additionally provides a cell classification feature, providing a probability of a classifier categorising an ROI as being a true cell. We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells. We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters) for all subsequent analyses. To facilitate the use of Track2p with other preprocessing pipelines, we implemented a data loader that takes as input three simple NumPy arrays corresponding to ROIs, field of view and traces (for more information see: https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters).\nPreprocessing videography\n\nWe used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Interestingly, this sorting revealed a subpopulation of neurons that were highly correlated with the animal’s motion at later, but not earlier developmental stages (Fig. 5A     , see Fig. S4A      for all days). We first quantified this relationship by computing the temporal correlation between the first principal component of neuronal activity with the animal’s motion. Interestingly, this correlation showed a steep increase after P11 for most mice (Fig. 7D     ). This suggested that global fluctuations in neural activity are more strongly modulated by active movement at later than earlier developmental stages. Finally, we asked whether behavioral state could be decoded from population activity dynamics using regression analysis. Despite substantial variability, the same-day decoding performance increased with development (Fig. 7A-C     ). Since we tracked the same neurons across days, we could also probe the stability of this representation across days, by fitting a model on one day and testing it on all other days (cross-day decoding, Fig. 7E-H     , Fig. S7B      and C     15     ,35     ). Interestingly, this analysis showed that, once developed, this representation was indeed stable, allowing for accurate cross-day decoding, with the same neurons showing either consistently positive or negative modulation across days (see Fig. S7D      for the activity traces of an example cell with a high weight for decoding). Hence, by combining Track2p longitudinal cell tracking with decoding approaches, one can systematically map the emergence and stability of cortical representations in the developing brain. Discussion Here we described Track2P, a cell tracking algorithm that can be used to follow the changing dynamics of hundreds of matched neurons from daily calcium imaging recordings in the growing brain of living mouse pups. At the core of the method lies sequential affine registration followed by cell matching for each sub\n\nTERM Figure 7 43983\ned activity to multidimensional, behavior-state dependent neural dynamics. Beyond this key finding, our method opens a much needed tool for investigating the developmental functional trajectories of individual neurons during early postnatal brain development, and their deviations caused by genetic mutations or environmental perturbations. In the pipeline,\n        uses neuropil-subtracted fluorescence.\n    baseline : str\n        Method for computing the baseline of each trace. One of \"maximin\",\n        \"constant\", or \"prctile\".\n    win_baseline : float\n        Window in seconds for the max/min filters.\n    sig_baseline : float\n        Standard deviation of the Gaussian filter in frames.\n    fs : float\n        Sampling rate per plane in Hz.\n    prctile_baseline : float, optional (default 8)\n        Percentile of trace to use as baseline when baseline is \"prctile\".\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    F : numpy.ndarray\n        Baseline-corrected fluorescence, shape (n_neurons, n_frames).\n    \"\"\"\n    win = int(win_baseline * fs)\n    if baseline == \"maximin\":\n        # Flow = gaussian_filter(F, [0., sig_baseline])\n        # Flow = minimum_filter1d(Flow, win)\n        # Flow = maximum_filter1d(Flow, win)\n        F = baseline_maximin(F, win_baseline, sig_baseline, fs, \n                             batch_size=batch_size, device=device)\n    elif baseline == \"constant\":\n        Flow = gaussian_filter(F, [0., sig_baseline])\n        Flow = np.amin(Flow)\n        F -= Flow\n    elif baseline == \"prctile\":\n        Flow = np.percentile(F, prctile_baseline, axis=1)\n        Flow = np.expand_dims(Flow, axis=1)\n        F -= Flow\n    else:\n        Flow = 0.\n    \n    return F\n\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

Modified in place.\n    tau : float\n        Timescale of the indicator decay in seconds.\n    fs : float\n        Sampling rate per plane in Hz.\n    \"\"\"\n    NT = F.shape[0]\n    g = -1. / (tau * fs)\n\n    it = 0\n    ip = 0\n\n    while it < NT:\n        v[ip], w[ip], t[ip], l[ip] = F[it], 1, it, 1\n        while ip > 0:\n            if v[ip - 1] * np.exp(g * l[ip - 1]) > v[ip]:\n                # violation of the constraint means merging pools\n                f1 = np.exp(g * l[ip - 1])\n                f2 = np.exp(2 * g * l[ip - 1])\n                wnew = w[ip - 1] + w[ip] * f2\n                v[ip - 1] = (v[ip - 1] * w[ip - 1] + v[ip] * w[ip] * f1) / wnew\n                w[ip - 1] = wnew\n                l[ip - 1] = l[ip - 1] + l[ip]\n                ip -= 1\n            else:\n                break\n        it += 1\n        ip += 1\n\n    s[t[1:ip]] = v[1:ip] - v[:ip - 1] * np.exp(g * l[:ip - 1])\n\n\n@njit([\n    \"float32[:,:], float32[:,:], float32[:,:], int64[:,:], float32[:,:], float32[:,:], float32, float32\"\n], parallel=True, cache=True)\ndef oasis_matrix(F, v, w, t, l, s, tau, fs):\n    \"\"\"\n    Spike deconvolution on many neurons parallelized with prange.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    F : numpy.ndarray\n        Fluorescence traces, shape (n_neurons, n_frames).\n    v : numpy.ndarray\n        Pool values buffer, shape (n_neurons, n_frames).\n    w : numpy.ndarray\n        Pool weights buffer, shape (n_neurons, n_frames).\n    t : numpy.ndarray\n        Pool start times buffer, shape (n_neurons, n_frames).\n    l : numpy.ndarray\n        Pool lengths buffer, shape (n_neurons, n_frames).\n    s : numpy.ndarray\n        Output spike traces, shape (n_neurons, n_frames).
